## Page 1


Sharp error estimates in stochastic
homogenization of parabolic systems with
time-dependent coefficients
Jun Geng ∗a and Qiang Xu † a
aSchool of Mathematics and Statistics, Lanzhou University, Lanzhou, 730000, China.
May 19, 2026
Abstract
This article mainly proves the existence of stationary correctors under space-time spectral
gap conditions, which exhibit different properties from those of elliptic operator correctors.
Additionally, new flux correctors and their fluctuation estimates are introduced.
Based on
this, we obtain the optimal homogenization error in the sense of strong and weak norms on
C1 cylinders by using the duality and distance-weighted arguments, in which the (weighted)
annealed Calder´on-Zygmund estimates coupled with a novel form of the minimal radius are
developed. Throughout the paper, no small-scale smoothness of the coefficients is used.
Key words: Stochastic homogenization; parabolic systems; homogenization errors; annealed
Calder´on-Zygmund estimates.

Introduction
In recent years, Armstrong, Bordas, and Mourrat [1] considered uniformly parabolic operators with
random coefficients depending on space and time variables (with a parabolic scaling):
∂t + Lε := ∂t −∇· aε∇= ∂t −∇· a(·/ε, ·/ε2)∇,
ε > 0,
(1.1)
and they developed a quantitative theory of stochastic homogenization (e.g. homogenization errors
and large-scales Ck,1-type estimates) by assuming that the law of the random field a is invariant
under Zd × Z-translations and has a finite-range dependence, building on the ideas introduced by
Armstrong et al. [2, 7] 1. Additionally, Bella, Chiarini, and Fehrman studied (1.1) and established
qualitative properties of correctors along with large-scale parabolic excess decay estimates, under the
assumption that the law of the coefficient field a is stationary with respect to space-time translations
and ergodic, drawing inspiration from Gloria, Neukamm, and Otto [28]. In an almost periodic setting,
the first author and Shi [25] also developed a quantitative theory for (1.1) in compliance with its
elliptic counterpart by Armstrong and Shen [6, 45].
Regarding high contrast settings, Lau [35]
introduced a parabolic coarse-graining framework extending the recent work of Armstrong and Kussi
[5] on elliptic operators. The operators (1.1) also arise naturally in the intersection of homogenization
and regularity structure theory, as evidenced by the recent works [14, 31], while research on such
multiscale parabolic operators can be traced back to the classical literature [11, 56].
Compared with the quantitative theory of periodic homogenization investigated by the first author
and Shen [23, 24], the main challenge in the aperiodic case lies in the study of the following corrector
equation:
∂tϕj −∇· a(∇ϕj + ej) = 0
in
Rd+1,
with j = 1, · · · , d,
(1.2)
subject to an underlying ensemble of coefficient fields. However, to the best of the authors’ knowledge,
the existence of stationary solutions to (1.2) has not yet been addressed in the aforementioned
literature —a gap that is particularly relevant for optimal homogenization error estimates and further
applications. The main motivation and objective of this article are to close this gap by establishing
the existence of the stationary solution to (1.2) under a spectral gap inequality (e.g., (1.4)), adopting
1We note that Armstrong and Kuusi have already upgraded their quantitative theory under very general mixing
conditions in [4] compared to the previous monograph [1], which includes the spectral-gap type inequalities used in
this article. This line of arguments is now recognized as the coarse-grained theory [4, 5].
2


## Page 3


an approach that has been well developed to the study of quantitative stochastic homogenization
by Gloria et al. [26, 27, 28, 29], originally inspired by Naddaf and Spencer [40, 41]. The present
contribution is twofold: (i) we provide a rigorous proof of the existence of stationary correctors; (ii)
we demonstrate how this result can be utilized to improve the quantitative estimates in the stochastic
homogenization framework for parabolic systems.

Once the fluctuation estimates (1.8a), (1.8b), and (1.8c) in Theorem 1.1 have been established, the
optimal error estimate (1.13) follows from a dual method5, originally inspired by references [34, 49,
54]. However, the duality argument fails when d = 2, since the boundary layer phenomenon precludes
the possibility of obtaining a convergence rate even within the energy framework. Therefore, recourse
is made to the weighted annealed Calder´on-Zygmund estimates to accelerate the convergence rate
and overcome the negative impact caused by the boundary layer phenomenon. This idea was recently
developed by the second author and Wang for the optimal convergence rate of linear elasticity systems
on the perforated domains [52], and this method is specifically designed to address situations in which
the dual argument fails.
In the process of establishing the weighted annealed Calder´on-Zygmund estimates, the main
contribution is to introduce a new definition of the minimum radius, originally inspired by Gloria et
al. [28, 29],

Theorem 1.3 concerns regularity estimates in quantitative homogenization theory, which can be
traced back to the contributions of Avellaneda and Lin [9]. The first work on quenched Calder´on-
Zygmund estimate such as (1.17) was developed by Armstrong and Daniel [3]. Taking into account
the influence of boundary smoothness, the present estimate (1.17) was established for a stationary
and ergodic ensemble, which is novel even among results on the elliptic boundary value problems. The
weighted annealed Calder´on-Zygmund estimate (1.18) has proven to be very useful in quantitative
stochastic homogenization theory. For example, it can be used to study the fluctuation properties of
high-order correctors, homogenization errors, and boundary correctors (see e.g., [13, 50, 51]).
Although the complete proof of the above theorem is far from easy, the basic idea for obtaining
estimates (1.17) and (1.18) is conceptually clear: via the two-scale expansion, one has the following
decomposition:
∇uε = ∇uε −

Roughly speaking, V represents the “regular” part in which the approximating function ¯uapprox has
the same type estimates as the stated estimates (1.17) and (1.18). If we want to pass the regularity
of V to ∇uε, then it is required that Wε should be sufficiently small under the appropriate norm and
scales. Thereupon, the so-called minimum radius χ∗becomes relevant, and it exactly characterizes
the scale at which the approximating term Wε can be as desired small (see the concrete statement
in Lemma 1.4). We will implement this basic idea by using Shen’s lemma (i.e., Lemma 4.4), which
can be traced back to Duerinckx and Otto’s work [17] for random elliptic operators. To obtain the
estimate (1.18) from (1.17), the key idea is the repetitive application of Shen’s lemma.
In order to precisely describe this local approximation, additional notation must be introduced.

In stochastic homogenization theory, by appealing to the correctors, the oscillations of ∇uε can
be expressed via the two-scale asymptotic expansion as in the case of periodic homogenization:
∇uε ≈
 ej + ∇ϕj(·/ε, ·/ε2)

This is exactly the content of the estimate (1.19) at large scales (through the notion of the minimum
radius). However, as opposed to the deterministic case, the stochastic setting additionally involves the
study of the fluctuations of ∇uε alongside its oscillations. Concerned with random elliptic operators,
the fluctuations exhibit a central limit theorem (CLT) scaling O(ε

Here, it is emphasized that the two-scale expansion used for
parabolic operator differs from that given for the elliptic operator. Correction for the time variable

commutator of the parabolic operators has the same structure as its elliptic counterpart. However,
this by no means implies a similar conclusion for high-order homogenization commutators, and the
related issues will be discussed in a separated work. In addition, the following results are also an
important application of the weighted annealed Calder´on-Zygmund estimates.

1.2
Novelties and remarks on the main results
Remark 1.6. While the notation Q1(z) in (1.4) refers to a parabolic cube centered at z = (x, t) ∈
Rd+1, it is important to clarify that the variable t in (1.4) does not coincide with the time variable
in the parabolic system (1.10). The time variable in an evolutionary system possesses a directional
structure, whereas the spectral gap condition here does not encode such information.
The central idea of this article is to extend the sensitive estimates developed by Gloria et al.
[26, 27, 28, 29] for elliptic operators to the parabolic setting. Specifically, we employ functional
inequalities as the primary tool to quantify ergodicity, thereby enabling a precise quantification of
the sublinear growth of the correctors. A distinguishing feature of our results is that the existence
of stationary correctors associated with the parabolic operator (1.1), as established in Theorem 1.1,
is independent of the spatial dimension, which markedly contrasts with the dimension-dependent
results for elliptic operators. Furthermore, compared with the work of Bella et al. [10], a novel
structural refinement is introduced by expanding qij into (qij, q(d+1)j) as shown in (1.6). This endows
the new vector field q·j with two crucial properties: it is divergence-free and exhibits mean zero (see
Proposition 2.2). This structural innovation allows us to exploit the intrinsic geometry of the equation
(1.2), leading to the derivation of the new system (1.5). Although this observation was initially
recognized in the periodic case by the first author and Shen [24], the extension to the stochastic
setting and the establishment of the new estimates (1.8a), (1.8b), and (1.8c) require substantially
more sophisticated analytical techniques (see Section 2).
Remark 1.7. As noted in [28], the minimum radius χ∗introduced in this paper exhibits both qual-
itative and quantitative characteristics within the framework of stochastic homogenization theory.
While its rudimentary form was already present in [10, Proposition 1], this quantity was not formally
defined in the work of Bella et al. [10]. A key innovation of this paper is the introduction of the
extended flux correctors (¯σk, ˜σ(d+1)) used to define χ∗6. Below, we briefly take ¯σk as an example to
illustrate that this minimum radius is well-defined even under stationary and ergodic ensembles. By
virtue of ⟨|∇σkij|2⟩< ∞and ⟨∇σkij⟩= 0, it can be established that for each i, j, k ∈{1, · · · , d},
lim sup

(1.25)
(see [10, Lemma 5]), and the stationarity of ∇σ follows immediately from the second equation of
(1.5). The argument for ˜σ(d+1) is more involved and is deferred to Remark 3.6. It is desirable to
obtain the optimal stochastic integrability for χ∗(e.g., under stronger functional inequalities such
as the Logarithmic Sobolev Inequality (LSI)), though this direction is not pursued here. It is also
noted that Armstrong et al. [1] addressed similar issues in a relatively straightforward manner and
achieved the sharp stochastic integrability under the framework therein.
Equipped with the minimum radius χ∗, one can, roughly speaking, further perform large-scale
regularity estimates analogous to those developed by Armstrong et al. [1, 2, 7] or Bella et al. [10, 28].
6It is further reflected in the fact that the minimum radius χ∗defined here is more amenable to be quantified in
terms of stochastic integrability compared with the related construction in [10].
8


## Page 9


The core principle is to convert the (boundary) regularity estimates into a local approximation
lemma (e.g., Lemma 1.4) based on a qualitative (or quantitative) homogenization theory7. Since the
definition of χ∗relies entirely on correctors and flux correctors, its advantage lies in the ability to
“separate” large-scale estimates from quantifying the sublinearity of correctors. Although boundary
Lipschitz estimates do not appear in [1] or [10], they become tractable under the assumption of
quantitative ergodicity (see e.g., [2, 51]). Moreover, we pose the following open questions to guide
future research: Can boundary Lipschitz estimates be derived under stationary and ergodic ensembles?
Similarly, for a Lipschitz cylinder, can the estimate (1.17) be obtained as the same in Theorem 1.3?
Remark 1.8. Another effort of this paper is the establishment of homogenization errors under low
regularity of boundary geometries in Theorems 1.2 and 1.5. A key methodological contribution here is
the development of a unified framework that reduces error estimates to boundary-layer-type estimates
(encompassing both spatial and temoral boundary layers, see Fig.1) and co-layer-type estimates
for the homogenized solution (see Lemma B.4), even for domains with minimal regularity. This
approach not only connects to the study of low-regularity boundary value problems [12, 21] but also
demonstrates that providing a detailed argument remains essential to fully capture this meaningful
connection, particularly given that low boundary regularity inherently leads to convergence rate losses
(see e.g., the estimates (1.13) and (1.24)). While extending results from C1 domains to Reifenberg-
flat-type regions (or from Dirichlet to other type boundary conditions) is entirely feasible, generalizing
to Lipschitz domains remains constrained by integrability index conditions (see e.g., [50, Theorem
1.1]). Notably, the dependence of the aforementioned conclusions (1.13), (1.22) and (1.24) on the
domain diameter (which limits direct extension to the whole space) is addressed here: with slight
modifications to the proof, this diameter dependence is stransformed into a weight function in the
norms as seen in (1.14). Overall, the whole-space estimate is more straightforward than the bounded
domain case, primarily because it avoids boundary layer complications.
Remark 1.9. The investigation of higher-order correctors not only carries profound theoretical
significance (see e.g., the connection to the Bourgain-Spencer conjecture [15]) but also has substantial
practical utility in numerical applications, particularly in analyzing systematic errors within the
Representative Volume Element (RVE) method [13] and in addressing the optimal artificial boundary
condition problem [39]. Through asymptotic analysis, the governing equation for the second-order
corrector can be derived as follows:
 ∂t −∇· a∇

(1.26)
By Theorem 1.1, the divergence term on the right-hand side of (1.26) corresponds to a stationary
field. Consequently, analogous to Theorem 1.1, there is strong potential for identifying stationary
solutions for the equation (1.26), which are independent of spatial dimension. However, extending
the methodology for obtaining second-order correctors to higher orders proves exceptionally chal-
lenging for the parabolic operators in (1.1). A core practical obstacle lies in the scaling equivalence
between the time derivative and the second-order spatial derivative, which currently lacks an ac-
curate and structured representation in formal expansions. Thus, the exploration of higher-order
correctors and associated homogenization commutators for parabolic operators remains an interest-
ing topic. Additionally, the correlation structure of (first-order) correctors and the variance structure
of homogenization commutators will be elaborated on in a subsequent paper.
7If one studies boundary Lipschitz (or Schauder) estimates, a quantitative statement analogous to [51, Lemma 2.8]
seems to be inevitable, which could be observed even in a periodic setting (see [43]).

## Page 10


1.3
Outline of the paper
Although this article encompasses a significant amount of material, its primary line of reasoning
follows a structured progression. As shown in Tab.1, Theorems 1.1 and 1.3 form the most fundamental
theories of this article, while Theorems 1.2 and 1.5 serve as crucial bridges connecting to applied
fields such as computational mathematics.
Section 2 is dedicated to the study of the corrector and flux correctors, and the proof structure
of Theorem 1.1 is shown in Fig.3. Section 3 investigates two-scale asymptotic expansions, weighted
estimates of smoothing operators, and the proof of Lemma 1.4, which leads to the definition of the
minimum radius χ∗, as depicted in (1.15). Section 4 focuses on Calder´on-Zygmund theory, and the
proof flowchart for Theorem 1.3 is presented in Fig.4. Section 5 examines the homogenization errors
in the sense of oscillation and fluctuation, and the structural proof of Theorems 1.2 and 1.5 are
provided in Figs.5 and 6, respectively.

An apology is offered at the outset for introducing a substantial amount of notation here. This is
particularly necessary because the analysis of parabolic equations requires the definition of more
intricate symbols than their elliptic counterparts, and therefore it is recommended that this section

are used to indicate the referenced formula or estimate. The notation ∼is used when
both ≲and ≳hold.

Sensitive estimates
This section is primarily concerned with establishing fluctuation estimates for correctors and flux-
correctors by combining the spectral gap condition (1.4) with sensitive estimate techniques. The
key to the proof lies in the existence of stationary correctors, which is based on uniform sensitive
estimates for the massive correctors. The conclusion is drawn from the following propositions.

2.1
Proof of Proposition 2.1
The basic idea and key steps of the proof are explained in its first step and are therefore not discussed
separately. The novelty of the proof is also primarily reflected in the technical aspects of the detailed
demonstration. The entire proof is completed in seven steps.

2.2
Proof of Proposition 2.2
The proof is divided into qualitative and quantitative parts. We begin with the qualitative analysis,
employing spectral representations and functional calculus (see the pioneering work of Papanicolaou
and Varadhan [42]), as well as applying the established results of Bella et al. [10]. Subsequently, we
utilize the spectral gap condition (1.4) to investigate the fluctuation properties of the flux correctors.

2.3
Proof of Proposition 2.3
The idea of the proof is to repeatedly use a real method developed by Shen [44], and we refer the reader
to Subsection 4.1 for the main idea of utilizing Shen’s real methods to address the annealed Calder´on-
Zygmund estimates in a random setting, originally developed in [17]. Therefore, it is suggested that
readers who are not familiar with this method skip this proof during their first reading. We first
address the estimate (2.13) in the case 0 < 2 −p ≪1, and the counterpart case 0 < p −2 ≪1

Proof. Apart from the parabolic scaling and the parabolic distance, the definition of a parabolic
cube is analogous to that of a standard cube in Rd+1. Consequently, the proof of this lemma can be
entirely derived by replicating the proof of [50, Lemma 2.12], and it is omitted here. The idea can
also be found in [17].

Basic ideas: The repeated application of Shen’s lemma (i.e., Lemma 4.4) is reflected in two aspects.
a. From unweighted estimates to weighted ones. Roughly speaking, the proof begins by
establishing the estimate (4.3) in the case ω = 1 (i.e., the estimate (1.17)) through the use
of Shen’s lemma, where the energy estimate plays a fundamental role as one of “inputs” and
accordingly p0 = 2 is taken therein. Then, the desired estimate (4.3) for Ap weights follows
from reusing Shen’s lemma, where the role played by the energy estimate is replaced by the
estimate obtained in the previous step, and accordingly the range of p0 will be greatly relaxed.
b. From quenched estimates to annealed ones (as shown in the flow of the proofs in Fig.4).
Understanding how Shen’s lemma is concretely utilized to address the Calder´on-Zygmund esti-
mates is crucial for the proof of Theorem 1.3 (or Proposition 4.2). The estimate (1.17) is taken as
an example to illustrate it.
Step A. Decompose the domain ΩT and reduce the desired estimate (1.17) to local estimates. Let
B0 ⊂Rd be a fixed ball centered at the origin with radius satisfying rB0 ∼R0 if R0 < ∞; or rB0 ∼1
if R0 = ∞. The basic parabolic cube is denoted by Q0 := B0 × I−rB0. For any z ∈Rd+1 one can set
Q0(z) := Q0 + z. Then, two families of center points are introduced: I for interior points and B for
boundary points. They satisfy the following properties:

Oscillation and fluctuation of homogenization errors
This section is primarily devoted to presenting the proofs of Theorems 1.2 and 1.5. If readers want
to have a prior understanding of the proof structure, they are advised to refer to Subsection 5.4.
5.1
A duality argument
Recall that 0H1,1/2(∂qΩT) denotes the Hilbert space of functions possessing one spatial derivative and
half of a time derivative in L2(∂qΩT), with each element required to vanish on ∂Ω×{t = 0} (see e.g.,
[12, pp.353]). It is noted that the following conclusion also holds for nonzero initial values (such as
H1
0(Ω) data), but requires a more delicate analysis.

Proof. The basic idea is to apply the weighted annealed Calder´on-Zygmund estimates stated in
Theorem 1.3 to accelerate the convergence rates near the lateral region. However, this method does
not easily handle the loss in the time-layer caused by the truncated initial value, which is an aspect
quite different from the duality argument. Therefore, the assumption of uε(·, 0) = u0(·, 0) = 0 is
essential here13. It suffices to prove the stated estimate (5.35) for p ≥q; the case p ∈[1, q) then
follows directly from H¨older’s inequality. The proof is similarly divided into three steps.
13This enables us to conveniently extend the solution in the time direction, thereby avoiding the discussion on time
layers.

which are also similar to that given in [50, Theorem 1.5], originally inspired by [33, Proposition 6.1].
The main contribution here lies in the technical aspect of constructing auxiliary equations suitable
for parabolic systems to achieve the goal of the optimal estimates. Let s, s′ > 1 be given as in the
proposition above, and the whole proof is divided into four steps.

Proof. The proofs are standard (since included in [1, 8]) and detailed proofs are not provided here.
Roughly speaking, based on (B.1a), one can first establish (B.13) for p0 = 2 by using Gehring-type
lemma (see e.g., [1, Lemma B.5]). Then, a convexity argument (see [22, pp.173]) leads to the cases
p0 ∈(0, 2), and this method is independent of PDEs. By the same token, one can have the boundary
estimate (B.14). The estimate (B.15) consequently follows from (B.13).
